e7 = (5, 7).
d7 = (4, 7).

Yes, (4, 7) and (6, 7) are not adjacent (distance = 2).

But e7d7 gains the defense of c7.
Is there any other move?

Pawn at c7 is already blocking the queen at a7.
The threat is Qxc7.
If I move the King to d7, the King now defends c7.
Now White cannot play Qxc7.
White's best response might be Bxf7.
But after Bxf7, the King is still on d7 and c7 is defended.
This seems better than letting the Queen enter the position.

White has a bishop on c4 and queen on a7.
The queen can move to b7, and if Black played d7, then the Queen on b7 would attack the king on d7?
b7 is (2, 7). King on d7 is (4, 7).
Distance is 2. Not a check.
The Queen could move to b7 and then play... what?
But e7d7 seems like the most logical move to stop the immediate threat of Qxc7+.
Actually, e7d7 also prevents the queen from moving to d7.

But the Bishop is on f8. To move, it needs g7 or h6.
f7 is still there.
So the Bishop is still trapped.
But the first priority is the King's safety and stopping Qxc7.

f8g7: White plays Qxc7+.

e7d8: White plays Qxc7+.

h7h6: White plays Qxc7+.
h7h5: White plays Qxc7+.
So the only moves that don't lead to an immediate check by Qxc7 (or at least don't allow it for free) are e7d7 and e7e8.
And e7d7 is the only move that actually defends the pawn on c7.

I'll choose e7d7.

Wait, if the King is already at f8, he must have moved there from e8.
If he moved from e8, he cannot castle.
So f8g8 is just a regular king move.
Same for h8g8.
Everything seems correct.
I will go with f8g8.
